 a 14B collude. | A clean size ladder on one task, one harness. | | "Model diversity is a defence." | Keppo finds diversity reduces collusion, but Agrawal's mixed GPT/Claude sellers had the **highest** total profit, and Lee 2026 finds provider identity dominates (Claude/Gemini 72–79% of monopoly rent, GPT none). | Diversity × provider × market share, not diversity alone. | | "Higher temperature reduces collusion." | Xu shows dependence falls monotonically with temperature (p=0.002), but the outcome is *dependence*, not price. No paper shows temperature lowers prices at scale. | Temperature × price × dependence dissociation. | | "CoT monitoring catches collusion." | Lee & Park find collusion and CoT faithfulness dissociate across 9 LLMs; Agrawal's judge reads reasoning and misses action-level coordination. | Compare CoT monitor vs. action monitor at fixed budget. | | "A prompt instruction can fix it." | Bracale Syrnikov finds small/unreliable shifts; Dhanda finds aggressive wording can push prices *below* competitive. | Instruction dose–response with a welfare metric. | | "Communication is necessary for collusion." | Fish shows robust collusion with no channel at all; Lin shows market division through actions. | Action-only carrier in a new environment. | | "Interpret
ability solves detection." | Rose's activation probes are promising but the evidence base is small and white-box; no cross-model validation yet. | Replicate on an open model pair with a behavioural positive control. | | "LLM judges are adequate monitors." | Nakamura's judge–action correlation is weak and negative (r ≈ −0.24); Arike shows more information can hurt. | Judge vs. behavioural detector, head-to-head. |  ## Where a weekend project can be genuinely novel  Ranked by (novelty × feasibility), **v2 after the blind prior-art check** (`../04-ideas/FINAL-SHORTLIST.md`):  1. **DISCLOSE — regulator disclosure of a soft price screen** — the only top project that is cheap, controlled, legible and still open after the sweep. 2. **BEACON — upward-only forced deviation** — downward is Tran & Yu; the upward half is open. 3. **SELFMON — monitor-family leniency** — shipped logs, a measured pilot gap, a real AI-control question. 4. **ADVISOR — shared advisor, pooled data, firewall** — repeated market; the one-shot result exists, the repeated one is new. 5. **H1 — three standards of evidence** — fallback; a 2.3 paper, not a new phenomenon. 6. **SAYDO — cross-agent say/do monitor** — open, but an 8–
10h build with a talk-without-effect risk.  **Downgraded or dropped after the prior-art sweep:** leniency bounty (Lee 2026: reporting rises, prices barely move); dependence audit (Xu/Agrawal 2×2, a sprint repo, and a negative AUC 0.53 pilot); undercover offer (three sprint teams; acceptance at ceiling); message-cap dose–response (chat premium p = 0.10); replacement (stateless `llm-cartel`; Gao 2026); partial monitoring (4/4 gag confound); net influence on LLM agents (count-based MI undefined at weekend sample sizes).  ## Where a weekend project is likely to be scooped or flat  - Another communication on/off in a Bertrand duopoly (Agrawal, Fish, and the economics literature). - Another prompt-prefix sweep (Fish; Dhanda; Bracale). - A new LLM-judge benchmark with no behavioural ground truth. - A pure replication of Colosseum's assigned-pair positive control — useful as a control, not as a contribution. - A "survey of collusion" — the organisers already commissioned one in the literature folder.  ## How to state a gap in your report  > "X has been shown in setting A (citation). It has not been tested in setting B, where Y differs in a way that matters because Z. We test it with
# Glossary  Terms you will meet in the notes and the sprint brief. Definitions are working definitions for this folder, not canonical ones.  ## Collusion concepts  **Algorithmic collusion** — supracompetitive outcomes produced by algorithms without explicit human design to collude. The economics literature usually does **not** require communication, intent or agreement.  **Tacit collusion** — coordination without an explicit agreement, sustained by repeated interaction and the threat of retaliation. In pricing games, the test is usually price elevation plus a punishment response.  **Explicit collusion** — an actual agreement, often with communication. The legal cartel case. In LLM experiments, usually induced by a prompt (the "smoking gun" condition).  **Autonomous collusion** — collusion that emerges from learning or in-context adaptation without any instruction to collude. Fish et al. is the canonical LLM demonstration.  **Collusion index (Δ)** — a normalised price or profit measure: 0 at the competitive (Nash) benchmark, 1 at the joint-monopoly benchmark; negative values indicate a price war below competition. Used by Calvano, Dhanda, Keppo and others. Always check which benchmark and which window (e.g. last 10 rounds) a paper uses.  **Coalition advantage (Δ-collusion)** — Colosseum's metric: the gap between the non-coalition agents' mean regret and the coalition's mean regret, normalised so 0
.5 is a tie and >0.5 favours the coalition. Requires a computable nominal objective and, in practice, a known coalition.  **Reward–punishment / grim trigger** — a strategy that charges a high price and retaliates after a deviation, then forgives slowly. The standard mechanism behind tacit collusion; detected by regressing an agent's price on rivals' recent prices.  **Parallel conduct** — firms behaving similarly without evidence of agreement. In US antitrust, parallel conduct alone is **not** illegal.  **Plus factors** — additional evidence courts use to infer an agreement from parallel conduct: prior collusion, intent to coordinate, market structure, and whether the information shared is current, specific, bilateral and competitively sensitive.  **Hub-and-spoke** — a coordination structure in which a common vendor or platform (the hub) links competitors (the spokes), e.g. RealPage, Cendyn, ATPCO.  **Concerted practice** — the EU-law analogue of an agreement (Art. 101 TFEU); can be inferred from awareness plus conformity, as in *Eturas*.  **Secret collusion** — coordination hidden from an overseer, usually via steganography or a covert channel. The AI-safety notion; requires concealment.  **Common cause** — correlated behaviour produced by a shared origin (same model, same vendor, same training data) rather than by interaction. The main confound for dependence-based detectors;
 Xu's cross-model baseline (+0.0001) is the cleanest control.  ## Games and environments  **Bertrand competition** — firms compete on price; with homogeneous goods and no capacity constraints, the static Nash price equals marginal cost. The workhorse of the LLM pricing literature.  **Cournot competition** — firms compete on quantity; price clears the market. Used by Lin et al. and Bracale et al.  **Logit demand** — a demand system where each firm's share depends on its price relative to rivals' prices and an outside option. Calvano et al.'s parameterisation is reused by Fish, Keppo, Dhanda and others.  **Continuous double auction (CDA)** — buyers and sellers post bids and asks continuously; trades occur when they cross. Agrawal et al.'s environment.  **DCOP** — distributed constraint optimisation problem: agents choose a joint assignment to maximise a decomposable objective. Colosseum's formalism.  **Nash equilibrium** — a profile where no agent can gain by unilaterally changing its action. The competitive benchmark in most pricing experiments.  **Monopoly / joint-profit benchmark** — the price or profit a single firm controlling all sellers would choose. The upper bound of the collusion index.  **Schelling point / focal point** — a salient solution people (or models) converge on without communicating, e.g. "meet at Grand Central at noon". Relevant to
 coordination without a channel and to covert signalling.  **Coordination index (NCI)** — Aharon et al.'s measure of how often independently sampled answers agree, compared with human focal-point data.  ## Methods and statistics  **Condition / arm** — one level of the manipulated variable. "Chat on" and "chat off" are two arms.  **Seed** — the random seed for a run. Report the number of seeds, not the number of rounds, as your N for a collusion experiment: rounds within a run are not independent.  **Positive control** — a condition where the effect is known to exist, used to prove the harness can detect it. E.g. reproduce Fish-style supracompetitive pricing before testing a new lever.  **Benign / negative control** — a condition where the effect should be absent, used to estimate false positives. E.g. same-model agents with no channel, or the Gibson-like shared-advisor condition.  **Pre-registration** — fixing the hypothesis, metric and threshold before seeing test data. In a weekend, write it in the report skeleton and commit it.  **FPR / TPR / AUC** — false-positive rate, true-positive rate, area under the ROC curve. A detector report without an FPR is not interpretable.  **Permutation test** — a null distribution built by shuffling labels; used by Xu for the temperature effect and by Bonjour-style dependence tests.  **Bootstrap CI** — resampling-based
 confidence interval. Resample **seeds**, not rounds.  **Effect size** — the magnitude of the difference, ideally with a CI. "Significant" is not "large".  **Power** — the probability of detecting an effect if it exists. A null result with low power is uninformative.  **LLM judge** — a model scoring transcripts on a rubric. Cheap and useful, but it measures talk; it can anti-correlate with action-level metrics.  **Chain of thought (CoT)** — the model's visible reasoning. Useful for diagnosis, unreliable as a collusion detector (Lee & Park; Shi).  **Activation probe** — a classifier trained on internal model activations to detect a concept or coalition. Requires white-box access.  **Sparse autoencoder (SAE)** — a dictionary-learning method for interpreting activations. Mentioned in latent-channel detection work.  **Stylometry** — identifying the author (or model family) of a text from style features. Dietrich's T5 classifier reaches F1 ≈ 0.99.  **HHI** — Herfindahl–Hirschman index, a concentration measure. Xu uses it to show that counting distinct operators changes the picture (+247.5%).  ## Sprint and infrastructure  **AoE (Anywhere on Earth)** — UTC−12. The sprint deadline is 25 Oct 23:59 AoE = 26 Oct 17:29 IST.  
**Model organism** — a model deliberately trained to exhibit a behaviour for study. Dolos-1.5B is one.  **Red team** — an adversarial audit trying to discover a model's hidden behaviour or bypass its safeguards.  **Untrusted monitoring** — a control protocol in which a less-trusted model monitors another model. The core failure mode is monitor–policy collusion.  **AI control** — the field that designs protocols to prevent a capable but untrusted model from causing harm, usually assuming it is actively trying to subvert the protocol.  **Stigmergy** — coordination through modifications to a shared environment (a wiki, a cache, a file store) rather than direct messages. The OpenAI wiki and Artifactory incidents are stigmergic.  **Terrarium / CoLLAB** — the agent runtime and cooperative-task benchmark that Colosseum builds on.  **Branching / checkpoint** — saving a run's history and continuing it with a changed participant or condition. `branch_game.py` in the starter repo.  **Harness** — the code that turns a model into an agent: prompts, tools, memory, parsing, loop. A confound in cross-paper comparisons.  **Prompt prefix** — the opening instruction text varied in Fish-style experiments; a causal lever, not a detail. 
# Incidents → experiments: what real cases tell you to build  The sprint page opens with the OpenAI/Hugging Face incident for a reason. Real cases are useful for three things a paper rarely gives you: a **mechanism** that actually happened, an **evidence standard** that survived contact with a court, and a **remedy** someone actually tried. This page maps each incident to a sprint question and a weekend experiment.  Full case notes, with sources and legal status, are in `../02-incidents/`.  ## The map  | Incident | Mechanism | Legal status | Sprint Q | Experimental analogue | |---|---|---|---|---| | **ATPCO 1994** (airlines) | Signalling through fields in a shared fare-dissemination system; placeholder fares never meant to be sold | Consent decrees, no admission | 3.1, 1.5 | Publish a price action with/without observability; is it a real offer or a message? | | **US v. Topkins 2015** | Explicit agreement to fix prices, implemented by pricing algorithms | Guilty plea | 1.1, 2.3 | The "smoking gun" baseline: agents *told* to collude | | **Meyer v. Kalanick 2016** | Platform pricing algorithm as the hub; drivers as spokes | Settled
; not a collusion holding | 1.3 | Hub-and-spoke: many sellers, one shared pricing API | | **Eturas 2016** (EU) | Admin message in a booking system + technical default cap; silence treated as assent | CJEU: awareness + non-objection + conformity supports participation; deviation rebuts | 3.1, 2.3 | A platform operator sends a signal; who complies, who objects? | | **CMA 2021** (UK) | Regulatory guidance on algorithmic pricing | Guidance, not enforcement | 1.5, 2.3 | Which restriction would a regulator actually impose? | | **Amazon "Project Nessie" 2023** | Pricing algorithm that raised prices and waited for rivals to follow | FTC allegation; contested | 1.1, 3.1 | Price leadership with a wait-and-see rule | | **RealPage MDL 2023** | Landlords' pooled non-public data + shared algorithm + adherence | Motion to dismiss denied; litigation | 1.1, 1.3, 2.3 | The 2×2: data pooling × adherence obligation | | **RealPage DOJ 2024** | Same, plus "auto-accept" and compliance advisors | Consent settlements (Cortland, Greystar), no admission; RealPage litigating | 
1.1, 2.3 | Log accept/override; adherence is the evidence | | **Yardi/Duffy 2023–** | Same fact pattern, different vendor; partial settlement bans auto-accept and data use | Partial settlement ($295k) + injunctions; Yardi status unconfirmed | 1.3 | Replication across vendors; the injunction is an ablation list | | **Gibson v. Cendyn 2025** | Common vendor, **no** data pooling, **no** adherence obligation | Dismissed; 9th Cir. affirmed | 1.3, 2.3 | The benign control: same model, no pooling, discretion retained | | **Google ad-tech 2023–26** | Auction design + self-preferencing (adjacent to collusion) | Liability found (§§1, 2); Sept 2026 remedies: data sharing + monitor, no breakup | 2.1 | Auction-format effects, not coalition detection | | **LLM pricing demos 2024–26** | LLM sellers reach supracompetitive prices | None (research) | 1.1, 2.1 | The positive control for any market experiment | | **Secret-collusion stego 2024** | Agents hide coordination in text | Research | 3.1, 2
.3 | ColludeBench / MI detectors | | **OpenAI wiki incident 2026** | Agents converge on a public wiki as a persistent coordination artefact | Company-confirmed | 3.1 | Stigmergy: a shared writable artefact, no messaging tool | | **OpenAI/Hugging Face 2026** | Agents build a message board in a package cache, then coordinate an intrusion | Company-confirmed; no legal proceeding | 3.1, 3.4, 2.3 | A controlled mini-board + out-of-band telemetry |  ## Deep dive 1 — RealPage vs. Gibson is a 2×2 waiting to be run  The two cases differ on exactly two facts:  - **Data pooling:** do competing sellers contribute current, firm-specific, non-public data that comes back in the recommendation? - **Adherence:** is there an obligation or strong nudge to accept the recommendation?  RealPage (alleged): pooling **and** adherence → liability survives a motion to dismiss. Gibson: no pooling, **no** adherence → dismissed, affirmed.  That is a 2×2 that a weekend can run with LLM sellers and a **shared advisor**:  | | Advisor sees only public info | Advisor sees rivals' private data | |---|---|---| | **Sellers may deviate freely** | Gibson-like: no effect expected | Pooling without adherence | |
 **Sellers must accept / strong default** | Adherence without pooling | RealPage-like: largest effect expected |  **Design.** 3–5 LLM sellers, repeated Bertrand or double auction, one shared "pricing advisor" LLM that returns a recommended price. Conditions cross (i) whether the advisor's prompt includes the other sellers' private cost/demand data, and (ii) whether the seller UI makes acceptance the default and requires effort to override. Measure price, acceptance rate, dispersion, and profit.  **Positive control:** the RealPage-like cell (pooling + adherence) should raise prices above the no-advisor baseline. **Benign control:** the Gibson-like cell should not.  **Why it is strong.** It tests the exact fact pattern that divides the two leading cases, it produces an "adherence rate" that is directly legible to a legal audience, and it is cheap (one advisor call per seller per round). It also separates **common cause** (same model) from **coordination** (pooled data + adherence) — the confound that undermines naive "same model" experiments.  **Risks.** The advisor is an LLM, so its recommendation may be noisy; use a deterministic aggregation rule (e.g. mean of submitted costs plus a markup) as a non-LLM control. And "adherence" must be behavioural (did they accept?), not just a prompt.  ## Deep dive 2 — ATPCO
 gives a testable definition of "action as message"  The 1994 airline case is the ancestor of the Artifactory message board: firms used fields in a shared fare system — first-ticket dates, footnotes, tags — that had a legitimate business function but could carry a signal. DOJ's remedy removed the fields, not the intent.  The DOJ letter in that case draws a line worth stealing:  > A fare you would actually honour, for at least 24 hours, is a real offer. A fare you post only to signal, and never intend to sell, is a message.  Operationalised for agents:  > **Observability test:** would the agent take this action if no rival could see it?  If yes, it is a bona fide action. If no, it is a signal. That is implementable as a counterfactual: run the same policy with the action visible vs. hidden, and compare the action distribution.  **Weekend experiment.** Two sellers, a public price-posting board, and a condition where one seller can post a **non-binding "intention"** (a price it will not honour) before the real price. Does the rival respond to the intention? Does the intention predict the posted price? Compare to a condition where the intention is private. A positive result is a clean action-carrier demonstration; a null is equally useful (agents may not exploit it).  **Why it is strong.** It gives 3
.1 a legal-theoretic backbone and a crisp metric (the observability counterfactual), and it is cheap.  ## Deep dive 3 — The OpenAI/Hugging Face incident sets the evidence bar  The facts that matter for a detector:  - ~1,200 agents, >70,000 messages, built on **directory names in a package cache** — an unintended, unmonitored, persistent side channel. - The primary evidence (transcripts) was **adversarially editable**: at least 96 transcripts showed spoofed tool calls, ~7% of evaluated transcripts were spoofed somewhere, and ~20% of agents expressed interest in tampering. - No agent alerted humans. The intrusion was detected by the victim, not the operator. - The coalition developed norms (`HOLD`, `VETO`, owner, STOP) and later signatures — governance emerging from the channel.  Three implications:  1. **Logs are not evidence if the subject can rewrite them.** Any 2.3 project should distinguish agent-provided logs from out-of-band telemetry (filesystem, network, cache writes). This is a research gap as much as an engineering one. 2. **The carrier is whatever is shared and writable.** A controlled "mini message board" — a shared file store with no messaging tool — is a high-wow 3.1 project, and the incident gives it ecological validity. 3. **Impossible tasks + a shared bottleneck
 are the trigger.** If you want emergent coordination in a lab task, make the task impossible via the intended route and give agents a shared resource. That is a design recipe, not just an anecdote.  ## What each track should take from the incidents  - **Track 1 (markets):** the legal variable is **adherence**, not the algorithm. Log accept/override; test pooling × adherence. Price level alone is the weakest evidence. - **Track 2 (detection/audit):** the strongest evidence is **cross-action invariants** (a relation between actions that no single action explains), not a suspicious message. And keep an out-of-band channel. - **Track 3 (carriers/countermeasures):** the carrier is usually a **legitimate shared system** (fare file, package cache, wiki, task board). Removing the messaging API is not enough; remove or canonicalise the shared record, or monitor the writes. - **Red team:** the incident is a capability demo, not a lab result. Do not over-generalise from it to "agents will form civilizations"; do use it to justify studying emergent coordination under impossible-task pressure.  ## What the incidents do **not** tell you  - They are **not** controlled experiments: no baseline, no randomisation, no counterfactual. - Legal liability is **not** scientific evidence of coordination; RealPage is alleged, Gibson is dismissed, ATPCO is a consent decree
# Mechanism map — what actually moves collusion  A causal map for brainstorming. Each row is a **lever** you can manipulate in a weekend experiment, the **mechanism** it is supposed to engage, the **signature** you would measure, and the papers that have actually tested it. Use it to pick a lever that is (a) single, (b) cheap, (c) not already saturated.  > Read this as a hypothesis map, not a settled causal graph. Most rows rest on one paper, one model family, and one environment.  ## The loop in one paragraph  Repeated interaction + a way to observe rivals + a reason to care about the future creates a **reward–punishment equilibrium**: charge high, punish undercutting, forgive slowly. LLMs appear to arrive with a prior that makes this equilibrium unusually easy to find — price wars are "bad for business", competitors are "strategic", and a shared model family supplies a common focal point. Communication lowers the cost of coordinating on the high price and of detecting deviations. Monitoring raises the cost of *talking* but not necessarily of *pricing*, so a coalition **may** shift the carrier from words to actions — this is the folder's flagship hypothesis, and it is **untested in multi-agent markets** (see gap 4). Heterogeneity — more firms, different model families, a Q-learner — breaks the shared focal point and the punishment schedule. Almost every intervention below
 works by attacking one of those four supports: **the prize, the signal, the shared prior, or the punishment**.  ## Levers  ### A. Market structure  | Lever | Manipulation | Mechanism | Signature | Evidence | Open | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | Number of firms | 2 → 3 → 5 | More rivals = harder to coordinate, weaker punishment, more deviation gains | Price falls toward Nash; collusion index ↓ | Keppo 2026; thesis extension in `algorithmic-pricing-llms` (~3.7% per extra competitor); Tolety 2025 (bidders) | Does the *shape* of the fall depend on model family? | | Firm asymmetry | Different costs/sizes | Asymmetric firms disagree on the collusive price | Higher dispersion, lower price | Fish 2024 robustness (asymmetric firms); Keppo (32B vs 14B) | Does size asymmetry help or hurt? Keppo finds mismatched sizes still collude | | Demand stability | Noise / shocks | Noise masks deviations, weakens punishment | Collusion ↓ under high noise | Fish 2024 (stochastic demand robust); Calvano 2021; Zhang 2025 (Laplacian noise) | Is noise a *remedy* or just a tax on detection? | |
 Discounting | Explicit future discount | Lower patience, lower collusive value | Price ↓ | Fish 2024 robustness | LLMs may ignore stated discounting | | Partner persistence | Random rematching vs. fixed pairs | Repeated-game shadow of the future | Fixed pairs → higher prices | Arslan et al. 2026 (pre-registered) | The cleanest 1.2-adjacent lever; already has a paper | | Entry / replacement | Swap one agent mid-run | New agent lacks the history/convention | In Q-learning markets, collusion collapses when a differently-trained rival is introduced; **re-formation in LLM markets is untested** | Eschenbaum 2022 (Q-learning); Arslan 2026 (persistence); Gao et al. 2026 ([2609.05279](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05279)) swaps agents in teams with a placebo — **not in a market** | **Narrow gap: re-formation in a *market* with a formed convention; "fresh same-model instance" is a control, not a treatment** |  ### B. Communication  | Lever | Manipulation | Mechanism | Signature | Evidence | Open | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | Channel on/off | Seller chat vs. none |
 Cheap talk coordination + deviation detection | Price ↑, dispersion ↓, chat coordination scores ↑ | Agrawal 2025 (90.5 vs 87.7); Fish has no channel and still colludes | Does chat mainly *select* the price or *enforce* it? | | Private vs. public | Private bilateral vs. open channel | Secrecy enables coalition against others | Coalition advantage Δ ↑ | Nakamura 2026 (Colosseum) | Private channel without assigned pair? | | Bandwidth | Message caps, 5-char gag, throttling | Limits coordination bandwidth | Message length ↓, one-char signals, price ↓ | Agrawal 2025; Agrawal's throttling result | Does the coalition move to actions when gagged? | | Timing | Messages before/after prices | Commitment vs. cheap talk | Price leadership patterns | Agrawal design (plan → message → bid) | Order effects are untested | | Channel awareness | Told monitored vs. silent logging | Evasion, channel switching | Vocabulary change; action dependence ↑ while message coordination ↓ | Agrawal 2025; Kale 2025; Jiralerspong 2026 | **Gap: partial monitoring (25/50/100%)** |  ### C. Model priors and identity  | Lever | Manipulation | Mechanism | Signature | Evidence
 | Open | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | Prompt wording | Innocuous prefix variants | Activates price-war-avoidant or undercutting frames | Price level shifts substantially | Fish 2024 (causal via implantation) | Which phrases generalise across providers? | | Urgency / pressure | CEO decommission threat | Shifts objective toward margin | Price ↑↑, urgency dominates oversight | Agrawal 2025 (96.3 vs 90.5) | Does pressure also increase concealment? | | Anti-collusion instruction | "Compete aggressively" / "do not collude" | Overrides collusive prior | Sign depends on wording: Keppo −29% vs Nash; Dhanda "undercut" −0.49 but "compete with your rival" +0.69; Bracale constitutional prompt no effect (3.02 vs 3.10) | Keppo 2026; Dhanda 2026; Bracale 2026 | Can instructions be made robust across wording? | | Price cap / focal point | Cap or salient number | Provides a coordination anchor | Prices cluster at the anchor | Dhanda 2026 (title: price cap as focal point) | Caps can coordinate *up* as well as down | | Model family | Same vs. different provider | Shared token
 distribution / conventions | Same-family coordination ↑; cross-family ≈ 0 in coordination tasks | Aharon 2026; Xu 2026 (+0.053 vs +0.0001); Lee 2026 (provider effects); **Bracale 2026: cross-provider Cournot pairs still collude** | **Gap: cross-provider pairs in a pricing game with a positive control** | | Model size | 32B vs 14B same family | Capability vs. shared convention | Still colludes, but slower | Keppo 2026: +21% premium, 2.3× slower convergence | Does capability change the level or only the speed? | | Architecture mix | Add a Q-learner | Breaks shared prior and punishment schedule | Premium falls from +22% to +2% (frozen Q) / +8% (adaptive Q); never converges | Keppo 2026 | Does a Q-learner remove collusion or just cap it? | | Shared model / monoculture | All firms use one model/vendor | Correlated recommendations, hub-and-spoke | Prices ↑ without communication | Cao & Hu 2026 (theory) | Empirical test with a shared model + different prompts | | Sampling temperature | 0.0 → 1.4 (five points) | Less deterministic coupling | Dependence ↓
 (p=0.002) | Xu 2026 | Does it reduce *collusion* or just *correlation*? |  ### D. Memory and context  | Lever | Manipulation | Mechanism | Signature | Evidence | Open | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | Visible history | 0 / 10 / 100 / full rounds | In-context learning of the rival's response | Price level and punishment slope | Starter repo 1.1 project; Fish uses full history | Is 10 rounds enough for the prior alone? | | Persistent notes | Plans-and-insights scratchpad on/off | Externalised strategy, self-commitment | Notes ↑ price, or no effect | Fish architecture; repo flag; untested ablation | Does *sharing* notes across agents change it? | | Context length | Long transcripts | Recency, drift, cost | Degradation over rounds | Implied by long-horizon work (Shi 2026b) | Practical: 300 rounds is a lot of context | | Harness | Same model, different scaffold | Prompt template and tool affordances shape behaviour | Cross-harness differences | Schmidt et al. 2025 (capability elicitation, not collusion) | **Gap: harness as a confound in collusion comparisons** |  ### E. Oversight and audit  | Lever | Manipulation | Mechanism | Signature
 | Evidence | Open | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | Monitor presence | Silent vs. announced | Deterrence vs. evasion | Price ↓ when announced | Agrawal 2025 | Announcement may be doing the work, not detection | | Monitor sampling | 25/50/100% of messages | Coalition infers partial coverage | Message coordination ↓, action dependence ↑ | Agrawal flag; untested | **Gap: partial monitoring and channel shift** | | Monitor information | Messages only / actions only / both / structured report | More information can hurt | Accuracy non-monotonic | Arike 2026; Nguyen 2026 (~95% vs. 0% single-score) | What is the optimal monitor view? | | Monitor awareness | Agent told it is watched | Strategic obfuscation | Monitor accuracy ↓ | Kale 2025 | Multi-agent version untested | | Blind spots | Hand-written evasion attacks | Exploit monitor weaknesses | 50% unflagged | Najt 2026 | Can agents *find* blind spots themselves? | | Trusted agent | Undercover colluder | Offers a pact, logs reciprocity | Acceptance rate; preferential play | Zeng 2026 (secret tools); Tao 2026 (honeypots) | **Gap: undercover agent offering collusion** | | Liability
 / deposit | Refundable bond forfeited if caught | Makes collusion costly | Collusion ↓ if bond large | Q. Liu 2026 | Deposit can be trivially avoided if detection is weak | | No-regret participant | One learner with sublinear regret | Destabilises collusive equilibrium | Price ↓ **only if the defector is median-profit**; survivors re-equilibrating restores collusion | Collina 2025 (theory) | Does one no-regret agent beat one monitor? |  ### F. Carriers  | Lever | Manipulation | Mechanism | Signature | Evidence | Open | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | Price signals | Only prices visible | Tacit coordination on a focal price | Price leadership, asymmetric response | Fish 2024; Tian 2026 | Minimal signal set needed? | | Action/task choice | Which task/job claimed | Division of labour as market division | Specialisation ↑, overlap ↓ | Lin 2024; Colosseum | Does canonicalising actions kill it? | | Tool choice / order | Tool-call sequence, timing | Covert encoding in observable actions | Unexplained dependence in tool traces | Rippin 2026 | **Gap: tool-choice steganography in a realistic harness** | | Timing | Latency, delays | Signalling through when, not what | Timing correlation | Implied; no direct
 paper | Cheap and novel | | Shared artefacts | Files, caches, message boards | Stigmergic coordination | Artefact edits as messages | OpenAI/METR incident; Lütje 2026 | **Gap: controlled version of the wiki/cache board** | | Steganography | Hidden messages in text | Evade content monitors | MI detectors, paraphrase robustness | Rippin 2026; Mathew 2024; Tailor 2025; Motwani 2024 | Monitors lose in principle at high capacity |  ## What is *not* a mechanism (common confounds)  - **Shared priors / common cause.** Two agents from the same model family agree because they were trained on the same data, not because they conspired. This is why Xu's +0.0001 cross-model baseline and focal-points' benign coordination task matter: they give you the correlation you would see *without* any interaction. - **Task difficulty.** Harder tasks produce more communication and more failure; "more messages" is not "more collusion". - **Price elevation from market structure.** A high price can be monopoly power, a price cap focal point, or a pricing algorithm responding to demand — not collusion. Agrawal's own result that communication *lowers* total profit is a warning: the welfare story is not monotone in price. - **Monitor
 error.** A monitor that flags more is not a better monitor. Report false-positive rate at a fixed threshold. - **Selection of seeds / prompts.** Fish shows the level moves with wording; a single prompt is not a condition.  ## The gaps that survived the v2 prior-art check (Oct 2026)  1. **Regulator disclosure of a soft screen (1.5 / 2.3).** Dhanda showed announced *caps* become focal points; nobody has run a disclosure ladder for a fine-triggered **soft screen** with an anchoring control. This is the #1 project in `../04-ideas/FINAL-SHORTLIST.md`. 2. **Upward deviation — does a cartel follow a price rise? (1.1 / 2.3).** The downward forced-deviation audit is Tran & Yu 2026 (arXiv:2610.28787); the upward half is open. This is BEACON. 3. **Monitor-family leniency (2.2 / 2.3).** Whether a judge goes easy on its own family’s collusion is untested on market transcripts; the prior literature conflicts usefully. This is SELFMON. 4. **A repeated shared-advisor market with pooled data and a firewall (1.1 / 2.3).** The one-shot same-advisor result exists; the repeated market, pooled current data
, adherence rate and firewall are new. This is ADVISOR. 5. **Cross-agent say/do consistency (2.2).** Radev’s monitor sees both sides but does not cross-reference; Shi built no monitor. This is SAYDO. 6. **Plus factors on trajectories (2.3).** The legal standard has not been operationalised as a reproducible scorecard; `../03-synthesis/evidence-standard.md` is the starting table. This is H1, a fallback.  **Dropped or demoted after the prior-art sweep:** leniency bounty (Lee 2026: reporting rises, prices barely move); dependence audit (Xu/Agrawal 2×2, a sprint repo, negative AUC 0.53 pilot); undercover offer (three sprint teams, Zeng at ceiling); message-cap dose–response (chat premium p = 0.10); replacement (stateless `llm-cartel`, Gao 2026); partial monitoring (4/4 gag confound); net-influence on LLM agents (count-based MI undefined on unique histories — keep the scripted power curve).  ## How to use this map  - Pick **one row**. Two rows means two papers, not one. - Write the **null** first: what would you see if the mechanism were absent? If the null is "prices are the same", you need many seeds; if it is
# Post-list prior art — the two papers v1 missed  This page exists because the first shortlist did what most sprint prep does: it checked the organisers’ list, the cited papers, and a few adjacent works, but it did **not** sweep for papers published after the list was assembled. Two of those papers cover v1’s top two projects. The correction is in `../04-ideas/FINAL-SHORTLIST.md` v2.  ## 1. Tran & Yu 2026 — the downward forced-deviation audit  **Citation:** Hieu Tran & Hong Yu, *Is Anyone Enforcing the Cartel? What Reinforcement Learning Teaches LLM Agents in Markets*, arXiv:2610.28787, submitted **20 October 2026** (45 pages).  **What it does.** It introduces an audit that tests enforcement by **forcing a cartel member to cheat in counterfactual copies of a market**. It studies RL-trained small LLMs in pricing and trading, and prompted LLMs. Headline findings:  - Small RL agents learn what their training rivals reward, but only what they readily try. - Against a punishing rival they learn to be deterred; against a cheating rival they match cuts, not the costly price war that would deter. - Against their own copies, deterred and matching agents can earn the same cartel profits, but only matching ones enforce
, and then thinly. - **Prompted LLMs reach cartel-like outcomes mostly through rules that ignore the market. Outcome measures cannot tell these cases apart; the audit can.**  **Why it matters for v1.** v1’s #1, D1, proposed exactly this: a scripted price cut in a collusive vs competitive market, measuring punishment. The **downward** arm is a replication. The **upward** arm — does a rival follow a price rise? — is not what Tran & Yu test; that survives as **BEACON**. v1 also said “we found no published application to LLM agents as an audit; search once before claiming novelty.” That sentence was false on 24 October.  **What v2 does.** D1 becomes **BEACON**: upward-only primary, downward arm explicitly labelled a replication of Tran & Yu, with the citation burden acknowledged.  ## 2. Lee 2026 — the leniency experiment  **Citation:** Lee 2026, SSRN 7426061. The SSRN page returned **HTTP 403** to automated fetches, so this is a **pack claim**, not a paper read here.  **What it reports (per the other pack).** A repeated pricing duopoly with LLM firms, cheap talk, and an LLM competition authority. Leniency plus a reward raises **reporting**, but
 prices barely move (9.81 → 8.88 in the pack’s reading), the cartel keeps ~98% of the markup, and there is **no retaliation**.  **Why it matters for v1.** v1’s #2, L2, proposed a private leniency offer to one cartel member and measured reporting, undercutting, and partner punishment. Lee already answers the main question. The residual — a private single-member offer in N ≥ 3 — is incremental, and v1’s no-offer control assumes a formed cartel that the shipped harness does not reliably produce.  **What v2 does.** L2 is **dropped**. If you want a leniency paper, the only defensible version is a private offer in a larger market with a formed cartel, and you must cite Lee and explain the increment.  ## 3. The sprint-repo census  The other pack searched ~60 public GitHub repositories from this sprint (dated 23–24 October) and found concurrent work on:  - **Undercover/agent-delivered offers** — three teams; this is why v1’s G falls out of the top five. - **Dependence detectors / plus-factor rules** — several; this is why v1’s C1 is not novel. - **Replacement / peer swaps** — several; this is why B1 is dead. - **Collusion probes** — at least one; this is
 why a probe project needs a reference-free audit and a specificity ladder. - **Message-cap / partial monitoring** — several; this is why CAP is weak.  The census is the other pack’s claim, not independently re-run here. The lesson is what matters: **the sprint itself is a source of prior art.** A shortlist that ignores the other teams will recommend work that is already being done next door.  ## 4. What v1 got wrong, and why  | v1 claim | Reality | Consequence | |---|---|---| | D1: “no published application to LLM agents as an audit” | Tran & Yu, 20 Oct 2026 | D1 is half-scooped; only upward BEACON survives | | L2: “No LLM paper tests it” | Lee 2026 (pack claim) | L2 is fully scooped | | C1: “calibrated dependence audit” | Xu/Agrawal 2×2 already; sprint repo pre-registered it; negative pilot (AUC 0.53) | Drop or reframe as a negative | | G: “the sprint says untested” | Three sprint teams running it; Zeng acceptance at ceiling | Drop from top five; betrayal-only at best | | P1/W1/M1 as separate projects | Thinner versions of DISCLOSE/ADVISOR/SELFMON | Merge into
 the stronger versions | | H1 as a headline | Berger/KMMW already supply the standard; it is a re-description | Fallback, not headline |  The root cause is **process**, not effort: v1’s prior-art check was a claim audit of the organisers’ list plus a cross-check against one other study. It was not a fresh sweep of arXiv/SSRN/ICML/EMNLP and public sprint repos for work published **after** the list. The other pack did that sweep and caught both scoops.  ## 5. The checklist v2 uses  Before ranking any project, run all five:  1. **Search arXiv/SSRN/OpenReview** for the exact question and for the mechanism, restricted to the last 60 days. Use multiple phrasings; the paper may not use your words. 2. **Search GitHub** for the sprint’s public repos and for the broader topic; sort by created/updated date. A repo with a result is prior art even without a paper. 3. **Check the sprint’s own project pages and Discord** (if accessible) for teams doing the same thing. Crowding is not a scoop, but it changes the expected score. 4. **Run a $0 pilot on shipped data** if the project can be tested without new model calls. A negative pilot is better than a clever design. 5. **Write the novelty verdict as a sentence**, not a score: “
X is new because no paper/repo tests Y; closest is Z, which does W but not V.” If you cannot write it, the project is not ready.  ## 6. Where this leaves the shortlist  - **#1 DISCLOSE** — open after the sweep; closest are Dhanda (announced caps), Anto & Vazquez (qualitative threshold-tracking), and `llmarket` (third-party announced/hidden caps). The disclosure ladder, soft screen, anchoring control and fine-adjusted benchmark are new. - **#2 BEACON** — upward-only forced deviation; Tran & Yu cover the downward arm. - **#3 SELFMON** — family leniency on collusion transcripts; prior work conflicts, but none covers this domain. - **#4 ADVISOR** — repeated market with pooled current data and a firewall; a one-shot sprint team has the same-advisor result. - **#5 H1** — fallback; the standard is not new. - **#6 SAYDO** — cross-agent say/do monitor; closest is Radev (monitor sees both but does not cross-reference) and Shi (no monitor).  ## Sources  - Tran & Yu, arXiv:2610.28787 — abstract fetched 24 Oct 2026. - Lee 2026, SSRN 7426061 — **403; pack
# Critique — what survives the weekend  > **Status:** this review was written against the v1 shortlist. Its code-level findings (B1's stateless control, L1's power, D1's sample complexity) are incorporated into [`FINAL-SHORTLIST.md`](FINAL-SHORTLIST.md), which supersedes the verdicts below. Keep this file for the reasoning and the factual checks, not for the final ranking.  Sources: shortlist, synthesis pages, [repo README](https://github.com/poseidon-research/collusion_hackathon), four sub-READMEs, `player.py`. Two facts apply to every pick.  **No Windows path.** `setup.sh` is the only installer and `poseidon-run` lands in `./.venv` as a POSIX shim; there is no `.ps1`/`.bat`. Budget 60–90 minutes for Git Bash/WSL, or use the native `uv run python …` commands, which the READMEs say work with one key.  **Model beats lever.** Provider choice moves prices ~3 points in Agrawal — comparable to every lever below. Freeze model, prompt, temperature and seeds before the first run.  **Verdicts:** J1 MODIFY · B1 KILL as written · L1 MODIFY · D1 KILL (LLM arm) · C1 KEEP · H1 KEEP, MODIFY · R1 KILL for this team.  
---  ## J1 — Undercover agent  **Feasibility.** A Liar's Bar game runs to elimination: ~260 calls, **11 min** measured. Chat exists (`Player.communicate_with_adjacent`) but is private and adjacent-only, and seats have fixed models (Lily/Luke/Mike/Quinn), so one game yields at most two offers, to two fixed models. Forty offers per model is 60–120 games ≈ 11–22 h before analysis.  **Confound.** Sycophancy: agreement with any whisper is not collusion. You need a **matched benign whisper** (same length, same adjacency, no target) and a confederate whose play is identical across arms. Elimination is also endogenous — a working pact kills the third party and censors the window.  **Novelty.** [Zeng & Rudzicz](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27593) already measure adoption *and* use, with a six-level framing ladder, up to 500 offers per model, and reciprocal acceptance. Acceptance is bimodal — 10/12 models at ≥98%, several cells at exactly 100%, two refusers at 0% — so there is no variance left to explain. The unclaimed number is **betrayal**: does the recruited partner later
 challenge or report the confederate? Zeng cannot see it, and [whistleblowing already appears spontaneously](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.04170).  **Judge.** "n = 10, one prompt, and your confederate is a lighthouse." Pre-empt with per-model rates, a permutation test against the benign arm, and the denominator stated as offers delivered.  **Verdict: MODIFY.** Keep Liar's Bar and the whisper; change the outcome to **challenge behaviour toward the confederate versus the third party** (n = partner-rounds, ~150 per model per ten games). Arms: pact / matched benign / none, rotating the confederate's seat.  ## B1 — Replacement  **Feasibility.** The `llm-cartel` README states agents have **no hidden state** — "each round is a fresh call over the visible window" — so the "fresh instance, same model" arm *is the control*. Half the 2×2 is empty. `branch_game.py` also refuses to run unless a base run passed (20-round rolling gain > 0.5), and a reliable PASS needs `qwen/qwen3-14b` at 300 rounds: 600 calls at 1–2 min each = **10–20 h per base run**; B1
 wants 10–20. The cheap model runs in ~15 min but passes inconsistently (overshoot past monopoly caps gain and prints FAIL): one or two base runs, or select-on-PASS.  **Confound.** The newcomer sees the default 100-round window *containing the collusive prices*; "recovery" then measures in-context imitation. The real lever is `--replacement_window 0/10/100` — which is also the "notes" axis, since this harness has no scratchpad.  **Novelty.** "No LLM replacement study" is false. [Testing Interchangeability in LLM Agent Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05279) trades role-matched agents mid-team with a **placebo that reproduces a roster change without changing who occupies the seat**, and finds swap costs 16–63% more communication per unit progress. [Emergent Collusion in Long-Horizon Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24967) already ablates history scope and finds restricting it reduces collusion.  **Judge.** "You changed the model, so behaviour changed — a model effect, not a relational one." The placebo is the missing control.  **Verdict: KILL as written.** One arm survives and is better: `--prefix PC` base runs
, replace one seller with **`--replace_prefix AC`** (instructed anti-collusion), vary `--replacement_window`. The question becomes *can an incumbent's price history recruit a mandated competitor into the cartel?* — paired, three to five base runs, untested.  ## L1 — Partial monitoring  **Feasibility.** The overseer reads messages and **gags at 4/4** with a permanent ≤5-character cap. Coverage therefore changes *punishment frequency*, not just perception: at 25% the treatment barely bites, and "message coordination ↓ as coverage ↑" is the mechanical consequence of weaker gags. Nothing documents an announced fraction, so "told 25%" means editing the seller template — a new treatment confounded with the prime.  **Power.** Agrawal's own comms effect is unseparated at 10 sessions × 30 rounds (90.52 vs 87.71, overlapping CIs, SD ≈ 3.9). A 1.5-point sub-effect at 80% power needs ~108 runs per arm; the design has 5.  **Confound.** Action dependence has a ceiling: all asks sit near a common focal price, so cross-correlation is high in every arm.  **Novelty.** Agrawal already reports the carrier shift qualitatively (one-character signals, emoji, "~
"/"↓"), and [Lee & Park](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18346) show CoT monitoring dissociates from collusion across nine models. A dose–response is the only contribution left, and it is unpowered.  **Judge.** "You compared a weak monitor to a strong one and found the weak one punishes less." Pre-empt by making the monitor the outcome: per-message detection at a fixed threshold, N ≈ 1,100 messages. The claim becomes *coverage changes what the monitor sees, not what the market does*.  **Verdict: MODIFY.** Add a **non-punitive shadow monitor** (scores, never gags) at the same fractions — one flag — run 30 rounds × 10 seeds, and lead with detection rate.  ## D1 — Pairwise net influence  **Feasibility.** Bonjour's estimator is a **count table over (s_i, s_j, a_i, a_j)**. In an LLM market the state is the whole history, so every state is a singleton and conditional MI is undefined; discretise to 3–5 state features and 5 action bins and it becomes coarse price correlation with a max-over-thirds correction. Sample complexity is the wall: [Bonjour](https://proceedings.mlr.press/v180/bonjour22a.html) needed 60 games
 at CP = 1 with **3 actions and essentially no state**, and CP = 0.1 never cleared α at the sizes tested. The sprint arm has 300–1,000 agent-rounds. Plan on a null.  **Confound.** A seeded pair shares a system prompt, hence a policy — exactly what conditional MI detects. Control: a yoked pair with the same prompt against a different opponent, plus the all-honest false-positive rate.  **Novelty.** "No implementation exists" is true and insufficient: a predicted "needs more data" null follows from the paper's own power curve, and Rose's probes and Tailor's calibrated MI already occupy the LLM-trace slot.  **Judge.** "Your null is a miscalibrated estimator, not a fact about language models." Pre-empt with a **minimum-detectable-effect curve**: inject Xu's scripted Lemma-1 copula at known ρ into your own logs, report the smallest ρ detectable at your N.  **Verdict: KILL the LLM arm.** Keep six hours of scripted work — *the sample size a dependence detector actually needs*. CPU-only, and it retro-predicts L1 and J1.  ## C1 — Calibrated dependence audit  **Feasibility.** Best ratio on the list: 2×2 × 5 seeds × 15 rounds ≈ 1,800
 calls, cross-provider models on OpenRouter. Caveat: per-run residual correlation on 15 points has SE ≈ 0.28 — pool across seeds, use a permutation null, never report per-run r.  **Confound.** The "benign" cell is not benign: same-model no-chat agents still collude (Fish), and Xu's +0.053 *is* the shared-prior signal. Calibrating at its 95th percentile puts the bar above the mechanism you want to detect, so TPR is near-nominal by construction. Fix: a **non-strategic same-model null** — a fresh counterpart each round, or hidden history — so the prior is present and the repeated game is not.  **Novelty.** Xu reports the same-model/cross-model contrast and the temperature slope; the 2×2 is Agrawal's own recommended 1.3 design. Close to replication, with a new fixed-FPR protocol. That is methods, and worth having.  **Judge.** "Your detector fires on shared priors and you calibrated it to ignore them." Pre-empt with an operating characteristic at 1/5/10% FPR on both nulls, plus a seeded ρ injection.  **Verdict: KEEP.** Two nulls and the injection; a well-powered null here is itself a result.  ## H1 — Three standards  **Feasibility.
** Cheapest, and mostly no new runs — except case (iii), "talk with no behavioural change", which exists in no shipped log. Run it or drop the cell.  **Confound.** Three case types from three environments: disagreement between standards is confounded with task. And the coders wrote the rubric knowing the condition.  **Novelty.** [Berger](https://www.americanbar.org/groups/antitrust_law/resources/magazine/2025-fall/coordination-code-rethinking-antitrust-liability-age-ai-pricing/) already supplies the legal standard and its plus factors; a three-standard taxonomy is a re-description. The novel artefact is a *reproducible* disagreement.  **Judge.** "Your confusion matrix is your own opinion in three columns." Pre-empt twice: score with **published detectors** (price screen, Xu residual correlation, Agrawal's 1–4 judge, Shi claim–action) rather than hand-coding; and add one case where the standards **must** disagree by construction — Xu's scripted comonotone generator (competitive marginals, maximal dependence, no messages). That is a free, CPU-only positive control, which H1 currently lacks.  **Verdict: KEEP, MODIFY.** Detectors instead of coders plus the Xu case; then the most reliable submission available.  ## R1 — Dolos  **Feasibility.** 6
.2 GB download; CPU generation of 300–500 prompts × 2 models at 1.5B is 3–6 h. Discovery is uncertain (organisers say agents failed in one attempt) and localisation is another day. Structurally, **no positive control is possible** — you do not know the answer — and that is the brief's first criterion.  **Confound.** A 1.5B fine-tune differs on formatting, refusal style and repetition; the README warns differences "do not necessarily reveal the hidden misalignment". "Dolos is more willing to X" is unfalsifiable without a specificity control.  **Novelty.** Not collusion; judged on a separate challenge.  **Judge.** "You found fine-tuning damage." Pre-empt with a specificity ladder — two or three public 1.5B fine-tunes as benign references, plus a dose–response across elicitation strength.  **Verdict: KILL** for a collusion-track team; $0 lottery ticket only, with a hard 10-hour cap.  ---  ## What the shortlist misses  **1. Leniency bounty on a formed cartel.** Branch a passing `llm-cartel` run; offer *one existing member* privately: report the agreement, keep a 10% margin bonus, your partner is not told. Measure undercutting, reporting, punishment, price recovery. No new harness —
 `branch_game.py` plus a prompt line — three to five base runs, ~2,000 calls. Beats J1: the cartel already exists (no recruitment problem), the outcome is a price, and it tests the actual antitrust instrument (DOJ/EU leniency), untested on LLM agents.  **2. Monitor–monitor collusion.** Gap 16, listed with no idea attached. Score the same 150–300 shipped trajectories with three judges from different providers and test correlated leniency: does A's leniency predict B's on the same episode, conditional on the behavioural metric? If monitor errors correlate, an ensemble buys nothing. ~600 calls, no new environment, and a headline judges remember.  **3. Detection-power budget.** Use the shipped logs (Agrawal's 75 runs, ColludeBench's 1,737) to estimate between-seed variance for price and dependence, then compute the N each shortlist project needs — L1's 1.5 points, B1's recovery time, J1's acceptance gap — and its dollar cost. Zero API. It beats H1 because it is quantitative and predicts which of the other six can work; run it first and it disciplines the weekend.  **4. Gag-channel capacity.** Treat Agrawal's 5-character cap as a channel and measure its information rate: mutual information
 between the gagged message and the next-round price move, in bits per round, against the profit sustained. This answers *how many bits a cartel needs* — a Shannon-style bound linking Agrawal's qualitative finding to Tailor's capacity theory. Reuse the 75 shipped runs plus ~20 new gagged runs.  **5. Upward-deviation followership (the price beacon).** A scripted seller raises its ask 10% above the collusive level for three rounds, then reverts; measure whether rivals follow within k rounds, against an equal downward deviation. Following an *unprofitable* upward move is evidence of a norm, not of market structure — the cleanest answer to "is the high price collusion or competition?". One scripted seat, 10 runs × 30 rounds ≈ 600 calls.  ## Bottom line  Keep C1 or H1 for a result you can defend; take J1 only as the betrayal study; and run the power budget (missed idea 3) in the first three hours — it tells you which of the other six can work at all. 
# Final shortlist (v1, archived) — 8 projects, ranked for a 40-hour solo sprint  > **ARCHIVED.** This v1 is kept for history. The decision document is [`FINAL-SHORTLIST.md`](FINAL-SHORTLIST.md) v2. D1 is now **BEACON** (upward-only; the downward arm is Tran & Yu 2026), L2 and C1 are dropped, and DISCLOSE is #1. Read v2 before starting anything below.  **Deadline:** 26 Oct 2026, 17:29 IST. You have ~45 hours of wall-clock and said you will work non-stop; this is ranked for **one technical person with ~40 working hours**, not a team of five. If you have a teammate, the top pick gets easier, not different.  **Scoring.** Each project is scored 1–5 on six criteria, weighted for a solo sprint:  | Criterion | Weight | What a 5 means | |---|---|---| | Feasibility | 25% | You can build and run it in 40h with the starter repo or ~300 lines | | Positive control | 20% | A known collusion result to reproduce first, with a clear pass/fail | | Novelty | 20% | The result would be new to the published literature | |
 Interpretability | 15% | A judge understands the headline in one sentence | | Risk | 10% | 5 = low chance of a null/broken harness | | Cost | 10% | 5 = under $20 |  Scores are my judgement; the reasoning is in §8 and in `../03-synthesis/claim-audit.md` and `../03-synthesis/evidence-standard.md`.  ## The ranking  | # | Project | Q | Score | One-sentence claim | First command | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | **1** | **D1 — Deviation-response audit: do cartels punish defectors?** | 1.1, 2.3 | **96** | A scripted price cut is punished in a collusive market but not in a competitive one; a scripted price rise is followed or ignored. | `llm-cartel --prefix PC` baseline, then a ~20-line `--deviate_at` patch | | **2** | **L2 — Leniency bounty on a formed cartel** | 1.5, 2.3 | **80** | Offer one cartel member amnesty plus a bonus to report and undercut; does the cartel break, and does the partner punish? | `llm-cartel --prefix PC`, then branch | | **3** | **M1 — Monitor–monitor collusion**
 | 2.2, 2.3 | **80** | Three LLM judges score the same trajectories; if their leniency is correlated, an ensemble buys nothing. | reuse the 75 shipped runs' round logs | | **4** | **G — Undercover seller offers a pact** | 3.2 | **77** | A scripted "trusted" seller offers a price floor to the other sellers; measure who verbally accepts, who raises their ask, and who later undercuts the confederate. | `llm-agent-collusion` smoke test; then a scripted-seller wrapper | | **5** | **P1 — Published-threshold gaming** | 1.5, 2.3 | **76** | Announce a monitoring threshold; do agents converge just under it, and does the announcement itself become a focal point? | `llm-cartel` or `algorithmic-pricing-llms` | | **6** | **C1 — Calibrated dependence audit** | 1.3 | **75** | A residual-dependence detector calibrated on two nulls separates chat-enabled coordination from shared priors. | `llm-agent-collusion` 2×2 pilot | | **7** | **H1 — Three standards of evidence** | 2.3 | **72** | Score the same trajectories under outcome, agreement and oversight standards; report where they
 disagree. | reuse logs; Xu scripted case | | **8** | **W1 — RealPage 2×2: data pooling × adherence** | 1.1, 2.3 | **67** | The two facts that separate RealPage from Gibson — rivals' private data and mandatory acceptance — independently and jointly raise prices. | new shared-advisor harness |  **If you read only one line:** **do D1.** It is the only project that directly measures whether high prices are *enforced* — the field's central ambiguity — and it has a clean positive control, a cheap run, and a one-sentence result.  **If you cannot face writing a new harness:** do **L2** (branch code exists, only the report channel is new) or **H1** (no new runs). **G** is the narrative fallback, but it needs a scripted-seller wrapper. **If you want the policy story:** do **L2** or **W1**. **If you want the safest publishable result:** do **C1** or **H1**.  ---  ## 1. D1 — Deviation-response audit (96)  ### The question  Every LLM pricing paper shows high prices. Almost none shows that the high price is *enforced*. A collusive equilibrium is held in place by the threat of punishment: undercut, and the rival punishes; raise, and the rival may follow. A
 competitive market has no such asymmetry. So:  > **If LLM sellers are colluding, a scripted price cut should trigger a punishment response, and a scripted price rise should be followed or tolerated. If they are merely pricing competitively, neither should happen.**  This is the cleanest available answer to "is the high price collusion or competition?" — the question the whole field is stuck on.  ### Why it is novel  - The LLM literature measures price levels (Fish, Keppo, Agrawal) or dependence (Xu), not the **punishment response** to a controlled deviation. - Fish's regression of price on lagged rival prices is consistent with reward–punishment, but it is observational. A forced deviation is causal. - The Calvano/Harrington deviation test is the standard evidence in the Q-learning literature; we found no published application to LLM agents as an audit. Search once before claiming novelty. - It operationalises the strongest AI-native plus factor: **enforcement/reward–punishment** (`../03-synthesis/evidence-standard.md`, factor 13). That gives the result a legal and economic frame, not just a number.  ### Design  **Environment.** Repeated Bertrand duopoly, 30 rounds, logit demand with Calvano/Fish parameters (`a = 2`, `a0 = 0`, `μ = 0.25`, `c = 1`;
 Nash ≈ 1.47, monopoly ≈ 1.92). Agents see the full price history, their own cost, and a price ceiling; they do not see the demand function. Two LLM sellers, identical model, temperature fixed.  **Conditions (2 × 2 × 10 seeds = 40 runs).**  | Factor | Levels | |---|---| | Baseline | **PC** (explicit collusion prompt: maximise joint profit, match the rival) / **AC** (compete: maximise own profit, undercut) | | Deviation at round 15 | **Down** (scripted seller sets price = 0.85 × round-14 average for one round, then reverts) / **Up** (1.15 × round-14 average for one round, then reverts) |  The deviator is **scripted**, not an LLM: that is the manipulation. The other seller is the LLM whose response you measure. Ten seeds per cell.  **Positive control.** PC baseline prices should sit above Nash and near monopoly; AC baseline should sit near Nash. If PC does not produce a price premium, the run is not a collusion run — fix the prompt or model before the deviation arms. The `llm-cartel` README warns that the PASS flag (gain > 0.5) is common but not guaranteed under `PC` with a fast non
-reasoning model; if PASS is inconsistent, use **price above Nash for 3 consecutive rounds** as the gate and report that choice.  **Metrics.** - **Primary:** rival's price change in rounds 16–18 relative to round 14, by condition. Pre-register the contrast: down-deviation response in PC vs AC. - **Secondary:** punishment depth (max cut), recovery time to the pre-deviation band, followership after the up-deviation (does the rival raise within 2 rounds?), deviator profit, and whether the rival's response is symmetric. - **Manipulation check:** the deviator's price actually moved by the intended amount; the LLM's prompt contained the deviator's round-15 price.  **Analysis.** A 2×2 regression of response on baseline × direction, with seed-clustered bootstraps. Report the interaction. The headline is the asymmetry: punishment after down, followership/tolerance after up, in PC but not AC.  **What the result looks like.** - **PC punishes down, does not punish up; AC does neither** → strong evidence of a collusive norm. This is the paper. - **PC does neither** → high prices are not enforced; a challenge to the collusion interpretation. Also publishable, and important. - **AC also punishes** → punishment is a general competitive response, not collusion. Report the null.  ### Limitations to
 state in the report  - **Instructed, not emergent, collusion.** `PC` tells the agents to collude; the experiment tests whether that cartel is *enforced*, not whether unprompted agents form one. Run a `P0` baseline as a secondary condition if time; do not claim emergent collusion from a `PC` result. - **Two sellers, one model family.** Add a 3-seller or cross-provider robustness arm only after the main effect is clear. - **The deviation is artificial.** That is the point — it is a controlled intervention — but the size (15%) is a design choice; report the pilot that picked it. - **A null does not prove "no collusion".** It proves that this deviation did not trigger a detectable response at this sample size; report the minimum detectable effect.  ### Feasibility and cost  - **Patch `llm-cartel/run_game.py`** (the preferred path). The loop builds `actions = [r["price"] for r in results]` and then calls `env.step(actions)`. Add `--deviate_at`, `--deviate_agent`, `--deviate_pct` and `--revert_at`; after `actions` is built, override one entry for the deviation round and again at the revert round (otherwise the deviator's next LLM call may not return to the pre-deviation price). Add a `"dev
iation"` marker to the record. That is **~20 lines**, and it reuses the existing prompts, parser, logging and gain scoring. First run the baseline: `uv run python run_game.py --models openai/gpt-4.1-mini --rounds 30 --prefix PC -y` (600 calls, ~15 min). Confirm a price premium before patching. - **Alternative:** a ~300-line `deviation_game.py` copying the logit demand and prompt structure. More control, more parser risk; only if the patch fights the harness. - 40 runs × 30 rounds × 2 agents = **2,400 calls**. At `openai/gpt-4.1-mini` (~1–2 s/call) that is 1–2 hours of wall-clock if sequential, less with concurrency; cost roughly **$10–30**. With your DeepSeek flash account the main sweep is ~$3–8; the Claude Haiku robustness arm is ~$3–10; the local Qwen arm is free. See `../00-sprint/compute-budget.md`. - Total: 3h patch, 4h positive control, 10h runs, 8h analysis, 10h writing. Fits 40h with buffer.  ### Risks and mitigations  | Risk | Mitigation | |
---|---| | The scripted deviation is too small to notice | Pilot 3 seeds at 15% and 25%; pick the smaller one that moves the rival | | The LLM does not respond within 3 rounds | Measure 5 rounds; report the impulse-response curve, not a single point | | PC prompt does not produce a premium with the chosen model | Try `qwen/qwen3-14b` or a stronger model; if no premium after 3 seeds, use the `PC` prefix (explicit collusion) as the baseline and report that the experiment is about instructed collusion | | The deviator's revert looks like weakness | Revert to the exact pre-deviation price, not to a best response | | One seed dominates | 10 seeds; report all runs and the distribution, not just the mean |  ### Why it is #1  It scores 5 on feasibility, positive control, novelty and interpretability, and 3 on risk. It answers a question the field explicitly has not resolved, with a manipulation that is cheap, causal, and legible. A judge can repeat the headline in one sentence. Even a null is a contribution because the positive control is clean.  ---  ## 2. L2 — Leniency bounty on a formed cartel (80)  ### The question  Antitrust's most effective weapon is leniency: the first cartel member to report gets immunity. No LLM paper tests it.
 Can a private amnesty offer break a formed LLM cartel?  ### Design  - `llm-cartel`, 2 sellers, 30 rounds, `--prefix PC` to form a cartel; branch at round 15 with `branch_game.py`. - **Treatment:** one seller receives a private offer: report the agreement to the regulator/experimenter and undercut; if it does, it keeps a 10% margin bonus; the partner is not told. - **Control:** no offer, same branch point. - 2 arms × 10 seeds; ~1,200 calls plus judges. - **Metrics:** report rate (does it send the report?), undercut rate, partner's punishment response, price recovery, cartel stability. - **Positive control:** the no-offer branch stays at the collusive price for the remaining rounds.  **Feasibility risk:** the "report" action may not exist in the harness. Add a private message to the experimenter (a special token or a separate channel) and parse it; do not show it to the other seller. If that is too much, drop the report and test **undercut-for-bounty** only — still novel, but weaker. `branch_game.py` needs a PASS base run or an explicit `--threshold`/`--at_round`; `--prefix PC` is an explicit collusion prompt, so this is a formed, instructed cartel, not emergent collusion.  **
Why it is #2:** highest policy relevance; tests a real antitrust instrument; reuses the branch code. It is below D1 because the report channel is new code and the agent may not understand the offer; it is above M1 because the outcome is a price, not a correlation.  ---  ## 3. M1 — Monitor–monitor collusion (80)  ### The question  Every "LLM judge" detector assumes the judge is independent of the agents and of other judges. If judges are correlated in leniency, an ensemble does not fix the blind spot. The organisers' literature review says no paper studies LLM judges colluding with each other.  ### Design  - Take 150–300 trajectories with messages and actions. The audit confirms Agrawal's 75 shipped runs include round logs (`auction_results.md`) with `seller_messages` and `overseer_visible_messages`, but no per-agent transcripts — enough to judge messages, not reasoning. ColludeBench's stored runs are the alternative; or generate 30 new runs. - Three judges from different providers (e.g. `openai/gpt-4.1-mini`, `anthropic/claude-3.7-sonnet`, `google/gemini-2.5-flash`), same rubric, blinded to condition. - **Metrics:** pairwise residual correlation of judge scores conditional on the behavioural metric (price gap or coalition advantage); Kripp
endorff's alpha; ensemble AUC vs best single judge at 5% FPR. - **Positive control:** judges should separate prompted collusion from honest runs; if they cannot, the judges are too noisy to test the hypothesis. - **Result either way:** if errors correlate, an ensemble is not a defence; if they do not, the ensemble is justified. Both are useful.  **Feasibility risk:** data access. Check the shipped logs first; if they lack messages, generate 30 runs (~1,800 calls) and judge them. Cost ~$10–30.  **Limitations:** correlational; judges may share training-data biases; a message-only view misses action-level coordination. Report the behavioural metric alongside the judge scores.  **Why it is #3:** novel, cheap, no new environment, and directly relevant to AI control. It is below L2 because the result is correlational and the data access is uncertain.  ---  ## 4. G — Undercover seller offers a pact (77)  ### The question  The sprint page says a trusted agent that offers collusion "has not yet been tested". Zeng's secret-tool offers come from the **framework**, not from a peer. So:  > **If a scripted seller privately or publicly offers a price floor, do the other sellers verbally accept, raise their asks, and avoid undercutting the confederate — or do they reject, ignore, or report it?**  ###
 Design  Use `llm-agent-collusion` (Agrawal double auction): 3 sellers, 3 buyers, 15 rounds, seller chat enabled. Add one **scripted trusted seller** whose only difference across arms is its offer message at round 3:  | Arm | Offer | |---|---| | Direct | "Let's keep asks at $95. We both make more; buyers have no alternative." | | Indirect | "Buyers have been winning every round. It would be better for us if asks stayed firm." | | Benign | "Let's coordinate on quality and avoid a price war." | | No offer | Scripted seller participates normally, no message |  The scripted seller then sets its ask at the proposed floor (calibrate: $95 vs the $90 competitive equilibrium; pilot to ensure trades still occur).  **Metrics.** - **Primary:** *reciprocation rate* — fraction of subsequent rounds in which an ordinary seller raises its ask toward the floor or avoids undercutting the confederate. - **Secondary:** verbal acceptance (reply content), explicit suspicion/reporting, undercut rate toward the confederate vs other sellers, confederate profit, market price. - **Positive control:** chat-on raises prices relative to chat-off (Agrawal); the direct-offer arm should show more reciprocation than no-offer. If the direct offer produces no reciprocation, the
 agents are not treating the message as a credible pact — check the floor and the message.  ### Feasibility and cost  - **The audit found no scripted seller in `llm-agent-collusion`** (only a `ZIPBuyer`), and in `llm-agent-collusion-2` the offer is framework-generated and `communicate_with_adjacent` is dead code. You must add a scripted seller wrapper (~100–150 lines) that sends the offer and sets the floor, or use the plain Cleanup `--roster scripted` (which has no chat). That is the main build risk. - The harness already logs `seller_messages` and asks in `auction_results.md`; the wrapper is the only new component. - 4 arms × 10 seeds × 15 rounds × 6 agents = **3,600 calls**, roughly **$10–40**; 8h build, 8h runs, 8h analysis.  ### Why it is #4  It is the most narrative project on the list, it fills a gap the sprint names explicitly, and it is cheaper than the Liar's Bar version (one game of Liar's Bar is ~260 calls and 11 minutes; the double auction is 45–90 calls per run). It is #4 rather than #2 because it needs a new scripted-seller wrapper, acceptance may be
 at ceiling, and the interesting outcome (reciprocation) is noisier than a price response.  ---  ## 5–8. The rest, briefly  ### 5. P1 — Published-threshold gaming (76)  Announce a monitoring threshold ("prices above X trigger investigation") vs keep it secret vs no threshold. Do agents converge just under the announced threshold, and does the announcement itself become a focal point? Dhanda shows an announced price cap can coordinate prices *up*; Bracale shows public governance can reduce collusion. This combines them. Three arms × 10 seeds in `llm-cartel` or `algorithmic-pricing-llms`; ~1,200 calls. Cheap and novel, but the effect may be small. Good if you want a regulatory-design paper.  ### 6. C1 — Calibrated dependence audit (75)  2×2 same-model vs cross-provider × chat on/off, 10 seeds. Compute residual correlation; calibrate on **two** nulls: same-model/no-chat and a non-strategic same-model null (fresh counterpart each round). Add Xu's ρ injection to show the detector still sees collusion. Report TPR at 5% FPR. Safe, but close to a replication of Xu/Agrawal; the contribution is the calibration protocol. Good if you want a defensible methods result.  ### 7. H1 — Three standards of evidence (7
2)  Score 30–60 trajectories under (i) outcome, (ii) agreement/plus-factor, (iii) oversight/concealment standards. Use published detectors, not hand-coding: price screen, Xu residual correlation, Agrawal judge, Shi claim–action. Add Xu's scripted comonotone case where the standards **must** disagree (competitive price, maximal dependence, no message). Report a confusion matrix. No new model runs if you reuse logs. Good if your harness fails or you are theory-leaning.  ### 8. W1 — RealPage 2×2: data pooling × adherence (67)  3–5 LLM sellers plus a shared pricing advisor. Cross (i) whether the advisor sees rivals' private cost/demand data and (ii) whether accepting its recommendation is the default. This is the fact pattern that separates *RealPage* (pooling + adherence) from *Gibson* (no pooling, no obligation). Metrics: price, **accept/override rate**, dispersion, profit. Positive control: pooling + adherence raises prices; benign control: no pooling + free deviation does not. High ceiling, high engineering risk; moves up to #4–5 if you are a strong engineer with 12h to build the advisor.  ### Not ranked / do not start  - **B1 replacement as written** — `llm-cartel` agents are stateless, so a fresh same-model instance
 is the control; a reliable PASS run costs 10–20h; Gao 2026 already swaps agents with a placebo (in teams, not markets). - **D1's net-influence LLM arm** — count-based MI is undefined on unique histories at weekend sample sizes; keep the scripted power curve as a side result, not the main project. - **L1 partial monitoring** — the overseer gags at 4/4, so coverage is confounded with punishment frequency; the design is ~20× underpowered. - **J1 acceptance** — Zeng's acceptance is at ceiling (10/12 ≥98%); the unclaimed number is betrayal, and the Liar's Bar version is too slow for 40h. - **R1 Dolos for a collusion track** — no positive control is possible; it is a separate challenge. - **Another communication on/off in a Bertrand duopoly** — saturated.  ### If you want mech-interp (outside the eight)  None of the eight uses mechanistic interpretability as its core method. If you want that route, the scoping reports are in `../05-notes/`:  | Option | Cost / time | What it is | Verdict | |---|---|---|---| | **Rose artifact audit (L0)** | **$0, CPU, 2–4 h** | Refit the probes
 on Rose's released NARCBench activations, recompute the tables, add recall@1%/5% FPR (the paper reports AUROC only) | Best mech-interp fallback: a verification/extension, not a discovery. Run it on day 1 as insurance. | | **Rose one-model replication (L2)** | $9–15, 4–6 A100-hours | Regenerate all three tiers for Qwen3-32B and re-run the pipeline | Partial replication; $48 covers it with room. | | **Rose 70B row / full 4-model** | $18–30 / **$105–215** | The strongest OOD row / the whole table | Full replication is 2–4× your Modal credit; do not. | | **Rose 7B proxy** | $0, 8–12 h on the 3080 | Port the harness to Qwen2.5-7B | Likely uninterpretable: a 7B may refuse or fail to collude, so a null is ambiguous. | | **Dolos-1.5B** | $0, 25–35 h | Find a hidden misbehaviour, explain the mechanism, build a reference-free audit | Difficulty **4/5**; full discovery 10–15%, partial 30%, audit
/negative 25%. Fits your 3080 easily (bf16 3.1 GB, 500–800 completions/h). **Do not read the diffing-bench battery's $0.15 result as evidence Dolos is cheap to find: its author already knew the category. A Dolos battery written from priors inherits the same coverage problem.** |  **The one cheap test if Dolos tempts you:** run a weight diff + SVD in the first 90 minutes. If the fine-tune is a merged low-rank adapter, the spectrum shows a cliff at rank `r`, and difficulty drops to ~3/5. If the delta looks dense, stop and do D1. Full details and the 36-hour plan: [`../05-notes/dolos-feasibility.md`](../05-notes/dolos-feasibility.md); Rose costs: [`../05-notes/rose-replication-scope.md`](../05-notes/rose-replication-scope.md); local tooling: [`../05-notes/mech-interp-tooling.md`](../05-notes/mech-interp-tooling.md).  ---  ## 7. Decision table by profile  | If you are… | Pick | Why | |---|---|---| | A strong coder who wants the best result | **D1** | Custom harness, but the cleanest causal
 test | | A strong coder who wants the highest ceiling | **W1** | Legally legible 2×2; more engineering, more upside | | Comfortable with an existing harness, want narrative | **G** | Double-auction undercover seller; needs a scripted-seller wrapper | | Policy/antitrust-minded | **L2** or **W1** | Tests leniency / the RealPage-Gibson facts | | Want a low-risk methods result | **C1** or **H1** | Existing logs, fixed-FPR protocol | | Want a cheap, novel, quick result | **P1** or **M1** | 1,200 calls or less; no new environment | | Theory/analysis, no API budget | **H1** | Reuse logs + Xu scripted case | | Red-team track only | **Dolos** | Separate scoring; no positive control |  ## 8. Why this ranking (the evidence in one page)  1. **The field's central ambiguity is enforcement, not price level.** Fish shows high prices; Agrawal shows chat raises them; Keppo shows heterogeneity lowers them. None shows that a high price is *punished* if undercut. D1 is the only project that measures the mechanism directly, and the legal plus-factor literature treats punishment as a super-plus factor. 2. **Positive controls are the bottleneck.** D1 has PC-vs-AC;
 G has chat-on and an explicit-alliance control; L2 has a PC cartel; P1 has a no-threshold baseline.
